Asynchronous Global Protocols, Precisely
Kai Pischke, Jake Masters, Nobuko Yoshida

Abstract
Asynchronous multiparty session types are a type-based framework which ensure the compatibility of components in a distributed system by checking compliance against a specified global protocol. We propose a top-down approach, starting with the global protocol which is then projected into a set of local specifications. Next, we use an asynchronous refinement relation, precise asynchronous multiparty subtyping , to enable local specifications to be optimised by permuting actions within individual asynchronous components. This supports local reasoning, as each component can be independently developed and refined in isolation, before being integrated into a larger system. We show that this methodology guarantees both type soundness and liveness of the collection of optimised components. In this article, we first propose new operational semantics of global protocols which capture sound optimisations in the context of asynchronous message-passing. Next we define an asynchronous association between global protocols and a set of optimised local types. Thirdly, we prove, for the first time, the correctness of the most expressive endpoint projection in the literature, coinductive full merging projection . We then show the main theorems of this article: soundness and completeness of the operational correspondence of the asynchronous association. As a consequence, the association acts as an invariant that can be used to transfer key theorems from the bottom-up system to the top-down system. In particular, we used this to prove type soundness, session-fidelity, deadlock-freedom and liveness of the collection of optimised endpoints.

1
Introduction

1.1
Background

Multiparty Session Types (MPST).

Session types

[
54
,
32
]
are a type discipline for codifying concurrent
components. They give an abstract view of
sessions
, which
are structured communications between distributed peers.
Session types were first proposed in the context of
binary sessions
,
such as client-server protocols or dyadic interactions between two
communicating agents.

Multiparty session types

[
34
,
35
]
(MPST)
extend from two-party to multiparty communications,
allowing the programmer to specify a
global protocol

to coordinate communicating components.
Using MPST, we can ensure that typed components
interact without type errors or deadlocks, entirely
by construction
.
The MPST framework is
language-agnostic
, and has been
adapted into over 30 programming languages
[
57
]
.

In MPST, we first start from specifying a global protocol (called
a
global type
) which we then project
into a set of local protocols (local types).
Each local type can then be
refined
to another, compatible local type.
Once each endpoint program is type-checked against its refined local type,
the set of distributed programs will be guaranteed to be interacting correctly,
without type errors or deadlocks.

There are a number of methods to refine or optimise local protocols.
In this article, we focus on
the
precise multiparty asynchronous subtyping relation

\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant_{\text{a}}}}

[
29
]
, as a refinement relation.

A Global Type
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
projection
(
\upharpoonright
)
Local Type for
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}

[formula]
Local Type for
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}

[formula]
Local Type for
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}

[formula]
subtype
(
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant_{\text{a}}}}
)
Subtype for
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}

[formula]
Subtype for
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}

[formula]
Subtype for
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}

[formula]
typing
(
\vdash
)
Program for
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}

[formula]
Program for
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}

[formula]
Program for
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}

[formula]

Figure 1
:
Top-down methodology of multiparty session types.

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
denotes a global type, which is projected into the three
participants,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}
, generating local
types
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}

for each participant.
Local types are then refined to

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}^{\text{\tiny opt}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}^{\text{\tiny opt}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}^{\text{\tiny opt}}}
.
Three distributed programs
P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
,
P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}
and
P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}

follow.

Precise Multiparty Asynchronous Subtyping.

Subtyping
enhances the expressiveness of typed programs.
The original synchronous subtyping
[
27
,
19
]

has been extended to
accommodate
asynchronous communications

[
13
]
,
allowing the
anticipation

of
output actions
for message-passing optimisations.
We say that a subtyping is

precise
if it is both sound and complete–a subtyping relation
is
sound
if no typable program has a type or communication error.
It is
complete
if there is no strictly
larger sound subtyping relation. It is proven that
the synchronous subtyping relation is precise in both
binary
[
13
]
and multiparty sessions
[
28
]
.

In this article, we consider
asynchronous communication

which is modelled using infinite FIFO queues: output
processes put messages in queues, while input processes read messages
from these queues. Hence messages are ordered but non-blocking.
In asynchronous FIFO semantics, the types should
ensure not only deadlock-freedom, i.e., that input processes will
always find messages, but also
liveness
, i.e., that
all messages in queues will eventually be consumed.

Chen et al.
[
13
]
have proposed the
asynchronous subtyping relation
,
where soundness is formulated as ensuring asynchronous liveness.
The most technically involved but practically useful
subtyping system in this line of research
is proposed by Ghilezan et al.
[
29
]
, which presents the first formalisation of the precise
subtyping relation for asynchronous MPST. The relation
is defined based on a
session decomposition
technique, from
full session types (including internal/external choices) into
single
input/output session trees
(without choices). This session
decomposition expresses the subtyping relation as a composition
of refinement relations between single input/output trees and provides
a reasoning principle for optimising the order between
asynchronous messages. See
[
14
]
for a recent survey on
precise subtyping.

Figure
1
describes the MPST workflow.
We assume a set of participants (or roles)
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathcal{R}}
in the distributed
system.
We specify programs for each participant

\{P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathcal{R}}}

and a
global protocol (type)

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
.
To type-check the session

\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathcal{R}}}}{P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}
(which denotes
a parallel composition of
P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
)
against
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
,
we project
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
onto a set of
local protocols (types)

\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathcal{R}}}
from the viewpoint of
each participant
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
,
and synthesise
local protocols (types)

\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}^{\text{\tiny opt}}}\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathcal{R}}}
from each
program
P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
.
We say that
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
types
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathcal{R}}}}{P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}

iff
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}^{\text{\tiny opt}}}
is a subtype of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}

i.e.
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
can be refined or optimised to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}^{\text{\tiny opt}}}
.

Asynchronous multiparty subtyping
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant_{\text{a}}}}
allows
for “safe permutations” of actions,
enabling us to type a more optimised program
P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}

which conforms to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}^{\text{\tiny opt}}}
.
Once each program is typed,
we can automatically guarantee that a collection of distributed programs

\{P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathcal{R}}}
satisfy safety,
deadlock-freedom and liveness.

This top-down workflow
is implemented by the MPST toolchains,
Scribble

[
58
]
and
\nu
Scr

[
60
]
, which
check whether a given global protocol is well-formed, and
if so, generate a corresponding set of local types.
Building on
Scribble
and
\nu
Scr
,
several toolkits have been implemented.
For instance, the Rust toolchain
Rumpsteak

[
18
]

uses
\nu
Scr
to generate state machines,
from which
optimised
APIs are
generated using a sound approximation of
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant_{\text{a}}}}
.
We shall discuss the detailed related work on
theories and implementations of multiparty asynchronous subtyping
relations in §
7
.
In the next subsection, we
explain how we use the asynchronous subtyping relation for communication
optimisations, introducing a running example.

1.2
Ring-Choice Example

We explain our workflow by introducing
a running example which will be referenced throughout this article,
the ring-choice protocol
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}_{\text{\tiny ring}}

from
[
17
]
:

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}_{\text{\tiny ring}}={\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}{\to}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{\mathsf{add}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\operatorname{int}}}})\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}{\to}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}{:}\left\{{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\mathsf{add}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\operatorname{int}}})\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}{\to}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}{:}\left\{{\mathsf{add}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\operatorname{int}}})\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}\right\}}\\
\mathsf{sub}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\operatorname{int}}})\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}{\to}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}{:}\left\{{\mathsf{sub}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\operatorname{int}}})\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}\right\}}\end{array}}\right\}}}}}}}

(1)

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}
loop
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{add({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\operatorname{int}}})}}
alt
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{add({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\operatorname{int}}})}}}]}
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}[{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{sub({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\operatorname{int}}})}}}]}
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{add({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\operatorname{int}}})}}
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{add({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\operatorname{int}}})}}
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{sub({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\operatorname{int}}})}}
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{sub({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\operatorname{int}}})}}
continue as
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}

Figure 2
:
Message sequence chart for
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{\mathrm{ring}}}
(one unfolding of
\mu{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}
).

The global type
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{\text{\tiny ring}}}
specifies that:

1.

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
sends an integer
n
to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
labelled by
\mathsf{add}
;

2.

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
sends an integer
m
to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}
labelled by

\mathsf{add}
or
\mathsf{sub}
;

(a)

if
\mathsf{add}
is selected, it sends the integer
m+k
labelled by

\mathsf{add}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
, and the protocol restarts from Step 1; and

(b)

if
\mathsf{sub}
is selected, it sends the integer
m-k
labelled by

\mathsf{sub}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
, and the protocol restarts from Step 1.

Global types are semantically similar to more traditional
protocol descriptions such as
message sequence charts
(MSCs)
[
30
,
1
]
.
In Figure
2

we provide a MSC for the ring protocol,
corresponding to
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{\text{\tiny ring}}}
,
showing the protocol visually.

If we assume
synchronous interactions
as illustrated in
Figure
3
(a),
no data flow would occur from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}

and from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}

before

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
receives data from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
. This synchronisation is
captured by the local types which are projected from
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus\!\left\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{add}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\operatorname{int}}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&\!\left\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{add}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\operatorname{int}}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{sub}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\operatorname{int}}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}\right\}}\right\}}}}

(2)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&\!\left\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{add}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\operatorname{int}}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus\!\left\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{add}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\operatorname{int}}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{sub}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\operatorname{int}}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}\right\}}\right\}}}}

(3)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&\!\left\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{add}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\operatorname{int}}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus\!\left\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{add}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\operatorname{int}}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}\right\}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{sub}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\operatorname{int}}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus\!\left\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{sub}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\operatorname{int}}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}\right\}}\right\}}}}

(4)

where the notation
\oplus
is a
selection type
which denotes
an internal choice (followed by label and payload), while

\&
denotes a
branching type
, representing an external choice.

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}_{\text{\tiny ring}}

(b) optimised projection of
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}_{\text{\tiny ring}}

:
Ring protocol: Projected and optimised interactions
(from
[

]
)

Under
asynchronous interactions
illustrated in
Figure
3
(b),
assuming that each participant begins with its own initial value,

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
can concurrently choose
one of two labels to send the data
to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}

before
receiving data from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
,
letting
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
start the next action.
By applying asynchronous subtyping (
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant_{\text{a}}}}
), we can optimise

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}
to the following

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}^{\text{\tiny opt}}}
, pushing
the external choice behind the internal one:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}^{\text{\tiny opt}}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus\!\left\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{add}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\operatorname{int}}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&\!\left\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{add}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\operatorname{int}}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}\right\}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{sub}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\operatorname{int}}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&\!\left\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{add}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\operatorname{int}}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}\right\}}\right\}}}}

(5)

With process
P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}
typed by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}^{\text{\tiny opt}}}
, we can run
the ring protocol more efficiently (see
[
18
]
);
we make this precise in
§​

6
.
An overview of the history of asynchronous subtyping is given
by Chen et al.
[
14
]
, encompassing the theory and applications
of the relation.

Associations and Type Soundness

We say a set of local types
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}^{\text{\tiny opt}}}\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is

associated
to a global type
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
iff
they are refined or optimised by
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant_{\text{a}}}}
from
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
’s projection

\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.
This article proves the
sound
and
complete operational correspondence

between behaviours of
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}

and
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}^{\text{\tiny opt}}}\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.
This is the link between optimised
programs and global types in
the top-down workflow,
combining projection and optimisation into one step.

More formally, given a typing context

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}:({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}},\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}^{\text{\tiny opt}})\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}})}}
where
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}})}
is a set of roles in
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}
is the type of the queue for participant
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
,
then the
association
between
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}

and a global type
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
is defined as follows:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{a}}{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}}\text{ if }{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}\upharpoonright_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\ ({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma^{\prime}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}})\text{ and }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}^{\text{\tiny opt}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant_{\text{a}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\quad\text{ and }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\leqslant}}_{\text{a}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma^{\prime}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}\text{ for all}\ {\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}})}

(6)

where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\leqslant}}_{\text{a}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma^{\prime}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}
extends the asynchronous subtyping to the elements of each queue.
Here,
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}\upharpoonright_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\ ({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma^{\prime}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}})
is the
projection relation
, relating the global type
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
at participant
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
to a queue/local-type pair
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma^{\prime}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}})
that describes
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
’s local behaviour (formally defined later in Def. 4).
Once we obtain the soundness and
completeness of the association,
we can derive the
subject reduction

theorem and
session fidelity
of the top-down approach from
the corresponding results of the bottom-up system
[
29
, Theorems 4.11 and 4.13]
. The bottom-up system
does
not
use global types and their projections, but requires an additional
check that the collection of local types (i.e., a typing context) satisfies
a
safety
property
[
51
]
.

More specifically, we divide the steps to derive these results as follows:

Step 1

We define the operational semantics of
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}

(denoted by
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}\,{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\xrightarrow{\phantom{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\alpha}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}
, which states
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
moves to

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}
after one communication between two participants) and
a typing context
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}

(denoted by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\to}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime}}
, which states
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}
moves to

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime}}
after one communication).

Step 2

We prove
soundness
: if

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{a}}{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
can take a step,
then there exist
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime}}
such
that

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}\,{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\xrightarrow{\phantom{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\alpha}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\to}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{a}}{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}}}
.
Here,
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}\,{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\xrightarrow{\phantom{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\alpha}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}
(resp.
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\to}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime}}
) denotes a single communication step of the global type (resp. typing context), formally introduced in Def. 7. The actual result we prove (Theorem
12
) is in fact slightly stronger, but this extra precision is not needed for the subject reduction and session fidelity results.

Step 3

We prove
completeness
:
if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{a}}{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}\,\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\to}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime}}
,
then there exists
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}
such
that
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}\,{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\xrightarrow{\phantom{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\alpha}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{a}}{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}}}
.

Step 4

We define the typing rule for multiparty session processes
using the association:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash^{\text{\tiny top}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{dom}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}}\right)}}{\ ({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\ |\ {\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft h_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}})\ }}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}}\forall{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{dom}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}}\right)}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}})=({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}})\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{a}}{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}}

where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}
is a typing judgement to assign
type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
to process
P
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma}}}}
assigns type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma}}}
to a FIFO queue

h
(defined in
[
29
, Figure 5]
).

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
means
process
P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
is acting as
participant
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
, buffering sent messages in
its queue
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft h_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}
.

Step 5

We prove
the subject reduction theorem of the top-down system
using
the completeness of the association with
the subject reduction theorem of the bottom-up system

[
29
, Theorem 4.11]
; and

the session fidelity theorem of the top-down system

using the soundness and completeness of the association with
the session fidelity theorem of the bottom-up system

[
29
, Theorem 4.13]
.
We additionally prove that all projected typing contexts
are
safe
,
deadlock-free
and
live
,
hence, by
[
29
, Theorem 4.12]
,
the same is true for all associated typing contexts
(
Thm.

13
).
We give detailed explanations and proofs in §
6
.

1.3
Outline and Contributions

We provide the first sound top-down system
of the asynchronous multiparty session types framework, from
global types right through to projected local types, their
asynchronous subtypes, and all the way down to the processes they type,
including the most flexible form of projection. Importantly, we specify the
operational correspondence
between these levels, including
in the presence of the additional semantic flexibility allowed by asynchrony.
This enables us to prove subject reduction and session fidelity theorems for the top-down system.

Previous works have studied incomplete parts of this system, which are
disconnected from each other: the asynchronous subtyping relation
[
29
]
,
extensions to global type syntax to allow for en-route messages
[
3
,
11
]
,
the coinductive full projection relation in the synchronous setting

[
55
]
, and
the liveness in the bottom-up system
[
51
]
.

For completing the top-down system with

\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant_{\text{a}}}}
,
many critical components have been missing, up until now,
including (1) the operational correspondence between global types and
associated typing contexts (
§​

5.2
),
(2) correctness of coinductive full projection
for asynchronous session types (
§​

2.2
),
and (3)
liveness, deadlock-freedom and safety of local types projected from a
global protocol (
§​

5.3
).
We fill these gaps, and in doing so we introduce several new technical
contributions,
including a non-deterministic operational semantics for global types
(
§​

3.1
)
that captures the additional behaviours permitted by asynchronous reorderings;
the
\text{balanced}^{+}
well-formedness condition and associated
depth functions (
§​

4.1
);
forgetful contexts (
Defs.

22
and
24
)
which handle the non-determinism arising from asynchronous subtyping
in the soundness proof;
and context projection (
Def.

26
)
which lifts the projection relation to contexts.

Our major contributions are the soundness and completeness theorems
(
Thms.

12
and
11
)
which establish that the semantics of global types
(
§​

3.1
) can faithfully mirror the semantics of
any of its associated
typing contexts,
which includes the projected typing contexts
obtained via the coinductive projection
(
Def.

4
).
We also prove for the first time that typing contexts
projected from a global type under asynchronous subtyping
are live (and hence safe and deadlock-free)
through the liveness theorem
(
Thm.

13
).
No previous work has established liveness
for projections in the presence of asynchronous subtyping
and coinductive full merging.
We use this to derive the subject reduction and
session fidelity results in
§​

6
.

We provide an extensive exploration of global and local types
in
§​

2.1
,
including syntax, projection, and subtyping.
We define operational semantics for both global
types (
§​

3.1
)
and typing contexts (
§​

3.2
).
We establish the sound and complete operational relationship
between these two semantics in
§​

5
.

This paper provides a significantly
updated and corrected version of the proofs
of the theorems
from the extended abstract in
[
49
]
,
as well as new examples and detailed explanations.
Compared to
[
49
]
,
we have: (1) added full and corrected proofs for all lemmas and
theorems;
(2) corrected issues with the semantics of global types in
[
49
]
;
(3) added new technical devices and syntactic functions to simplify
the proofs; and (4) added new examples to illustrate our technical results.

2
Multiparty Session Types

This section introduces
global
and
local
types, together with
queue
types.
As in the work of Barwell et al.
[
3
]
, our formulation of global types includes special runtime-specific constructs to allow
global types to represent en-route messages which have been sent but not yet received,
and we give a novel projection relation (
Def.

4
) which extends the standard coinductive projection
[
28
, Definition 3.6]
to
asynchronous semantics by simultaneously projecting onto both local and queue types.
We follow this by introducing the
precise asynchronous
subtyping,
which will allow us to preemptively send messages.

2.1
Global and Local Types

Multiparty Session Type (MPST) theory uses
global types
to
provide a comprehensive overview of communications between

roles
, such as
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{s}}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{t}}}}},\ldots
, belonging to a set
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathcal{R}}
.
It employs
local types
, which are obtained via
projection
from a global type,
to describe how an
individual
role communicates with other roles from a local viewpoint.
The syntax of global and local types is presented below
where constructs are mostly standard
[
51
]
.

\begin{array}[]{r@{\quad}c@{\quad}l@{\quad}l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B}}}&\Coloneqq&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\operatorname{int}}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\operatorname{bool}}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\operatorname{real}}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\operatorname{unit}}}\mathbin{\;\big|\;}\ldots&\text{\footnotesize Basic types}\\[1.42262pt]
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}&\Coloneqq&{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}{\to}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}{:}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B_{i}}}}})\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{i}}}}}\right\}}_{i\in I}}&{\footnotesize\text{Transmission}}\\[1.42262pt]
&\mathbin{\;\big|\;}&{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}{\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}}{\rightsquigarrow}}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}{:}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B}}}})\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}}}\right\}}}&\text{\footnotesize Transmission en route}\\[4.30554pt]
&\mathbin{\;\big|\;}&{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}}\quad\mathbin{\;\big|\;}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}\quad\mathbin{\;\big|\;}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{end}}&\text{\footnotesize Recursion, Type variable, Termination}\\[4.30554pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}&\Coloneqq&{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&\!\left\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}i}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B_{i}}}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}\right\}_{i\in I}}\quad\mathbin{\;\big|\;}\quad{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus\!\left\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}i}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B_{i}}}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}\right\}_{i\in I}}&\text{\footnotesize External and internal choices}\\[1.42262pt]
&\mathbin{\;\big|\;}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}\quad\mathbin{\;\big|\;}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}\quad\mathbin{\;\big|\;}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}&\text{\footnotesize Recursion, Type variable, Termination}\\[4.30554pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma}}}&\Coloneqq&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\varnothing}}}\!\mathbin{\;\big|\;}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\left({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}},{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B}}}})}\right)}}\!\mathbin{\;\big|\;}\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma}}}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma}}}}}}}&\text{\footnotesize Empty Queue, Message, Concatenation}\\[1.42262pt]
\end{array}

Basic types
are taken from a set
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathcal{B}}
, and describe types of values, ranging over integers, booleans, real numbers, units,
etc
.

Global types
range over
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G},{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{i}},\ldots
,
and describe the high-level behaviour for all roles.
The set of participants (or roles)
in a global
type
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
is denoted by
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}})}
.
We explain each syntactic construct of global types.

•

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}{\to}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}{:}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B_{i}}}}})\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{i}}}}}\right\}}_{i\in I}}
: a
transmission
, denoting a
message from role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
to role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
,
with a label
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}_{i}}
, a payload of type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B_{i}}}}
,
and a continuation
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{i}}
, where
i
is taken from an index
set
I
. We require that the index set be non-empty (
I\neq\emptyset
), labels

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}_{i}}
be pair-wise distinct, and self receptions be excluded (i.e.
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
).

•

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}{\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}}{\rightsquigarrow}}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}{:}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B}}}})\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}}}\right\}}}
: a
transmission en route
, representing a
transmission

of the message
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}
which has already been sent by role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
but has not been received by role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
. This type
is only meaningful at runtime.
We require that self receptions be excluded (i.e.
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
).

•

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}.{G}}
: a
recursive
global type, where
contractive requirements apply
[
48
, §21.8]
, i.e. each recursion variable
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}
is bound within a
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}.{\ldots}}

and is guarded.

•

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{end}}
: a
terminated
global type (omitted where unambiguous).

Local types

(or
session types
) range over
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}},\ldots
, and describe
the behaviour of a single role. We elucidate each syntactic construct of local types.

An
internal choice
(
selection
),
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus\!\left\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}i}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B_{i}}}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}\right\}_{i\in I}}
,
indicating that the
current
role is expected to
send
to role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
;
an
external choice
(
branching
),
{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&\!\left\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}i}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B_{i}}}}})}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{i}}\right\}_{i\in I}}
,
indicating that the
current
role is expected to
receive
from role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
;
a
recursive
local type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}
, following a pattern analogous to
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}.{G}}
;
a
termination

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{end}}
(omitted where
unambiguous). Similar to global types, local types also need pairwise distinct, non-empty labels.

Queue types
range over
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma^{\prime}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma}_{i}}},\ldots
, and describe the type of queues storing buffered asynchronous messages:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\varnothing}}}
is the empty queue;

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\left({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}},{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B}}}})}\right)}}
is the type of a queued message being sent to participant
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}

with a message label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}
and a payload of type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B}}}
; and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma}}}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma^{\prime}}}}}}}}
is the concatenation of two queues.
We consider queue types up-to associativity and we allow queue elements with distinct destinations to commute
Def.

1
, this simulates the existence of a queue for each potential recipient. We take the convention that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\left({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}},{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B}}}})}\right)}}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma}}}}}}}
is a queue with
head
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\left({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}},{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B}}}})}\right)}}
,
which can be dequeued, and tail
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma}}}
,
and that enqueueing
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\left({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}},{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}^{\prime}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B^{\prime}}}}})}\right)}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma^{\prime}}}}

gives us
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma^{\prime}}}}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\left({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}},{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}^{\prime}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B^{\prime}}}}})}\right)}}}}}}

i.e. queues are read left-to-right.

Definition 1

(Queue Equivalence
[
29
]
)
.

We define the relation
\equiv
inductively on syntactic queues by:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma}}}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\varnothing}}}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma}}}}}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma}}}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma}}}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\varnothing}}}}}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma}}}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma}}}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma^{\prime}}}}}}}})}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma^{\prime\prime\prime}}}}}}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma}}}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma^{\prime}}}}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma^{\prime\prime\prime}}}}}}}})}}}}\\[10.76385pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma}_{L}}}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma}_{R}}}}}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma^{\prime}}_{L}}}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma^{\prime}}_{R}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma}_{L}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma^{\prime}}_{L}}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma}_{R}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{\sigma^{\prime}}_{R}}}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\left({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}},{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B}}}})}\right)}}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\left({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}},{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}^{\prime}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B^{\prime}}}}})}\right)}}}}}}\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\left({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}},{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}^{\prime}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B^{\prime}}}}})}\right)}}}\mathbin{\!\cdot\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{\left({{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}},{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B}}}})}\right)}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\end{array}

Definition 2

(Unfolding)
.

We define the unfolding operator on global types and local types recursively to unfold recursive types.
We define
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{unf}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}}}\right)}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{unf}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}{}[{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}}/{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}]}\right)}

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{unf}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}\right)}={\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
otherwise.
We define
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{unf}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}}\right)}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{unf}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}{}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}}/{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}]}\right)}

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{unf}\!\left({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}}\right)}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T}
otherwise.

Below we define sets of roles for a given global type.

Definition 3

(Role Functions)
.

•

We define
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}})}

to be
the set of participants
in
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}

by induction on the structure of
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
:

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{end}}})}=\emptyset
;

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}})}=\emptyset
;

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}}})}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}})}
;

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}{\to}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}{:}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B_{i}}}}})\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{i}}}}}\right\}}_{i\in I}}})}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\}\cup\bigcup_{i\in I}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{i}}})}
; and

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}{\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}}{\rightsquigarrow}}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}{:}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B}}}})\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}}}\right\}}}})}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{roles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}})}
.

•

We define
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{sRoles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}})}

to be the set of participants in
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}

that have sent an en-route transmission
by induction on the structure of
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
:

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{sRoles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{end}}})}=\emptyset
;

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{sRoles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}})}=\emptyset
;

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{sRoles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}}})}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{sRoles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}})}
;

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{sRoles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}{\to}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}{:}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B_{i}}}}})\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{i}}}}}\right\}}_{i\in I}}})}=\bigcup_{i\in I}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{sRoles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{i}}})}
; and

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{sRoles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}{\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}}{\rightsquigarrow}}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}{:}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B}}}})\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}}}\right\}}}})}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{sRoles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}})}

•

We define
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{aRoles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}})}

to be the set of participants in
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}

that may perform actions,
i.e. appears either as the receiver of a transmission
or as the sender of a non-en-route transmission,
by induction on the structure of
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
:

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{aRoles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{end}}})}=\emptyset
;

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{aRoles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}})}=\emptyset
;

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{aRoles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}}})}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{aRoles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}})}
;

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{aRoles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}{\to}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}{:}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}_{i}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B_{i}}}}})\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{i}}}}}\right\}}_{i\in I}}})}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\}\cup\bigcup_{i\in I}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{aRoles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{i}}})}
;
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{aRoles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}{\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}}{\rightsquigarrow}}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}{:}\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B}}}})\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}}}\right\}}}})}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{aRoles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}})}

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{sRoles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}})}
are the participants that have non-empty queues
once projected, and

{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\operatorname{aRoles}({\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}})}
are the participants that have non-terminated local types
once projected.

We will now use the ring protocol global type,
and an intermediate global type,
to demonstrate unfolding
and the role functions.
