as desired.
∎

Remark 8

(Subject Reduction Labels)
.

By examining the proof of
Thm.

28
,
we can deduce the labels of the context
transition sequence
from the reduction rules
used in the session reduction sequence.
For simplicity, we consider a single session reduction
as this implies the full subject reduction.
Suppose that
M\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\to}}}M^{\prime}\not\equiv{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{err}}}
and consider the
non-
[R-Struct]
rule deriving it.

[R-Send]

If the reduction occurred via
[R-Send]

at role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
with label
\ell
and destination
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
,
then

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime}}\,\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\mathord{:}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}\mathord{\oplus}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B}}})}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime\prime}}

for some ground type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B}}}
.

[R-Rcv]

If the reduction occurred via
[R-Rcv]

at role
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
receiving label
\ell
and with source
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
,
then

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime}}\,\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\xrightarrow{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\mathord{:}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}\&{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B}}})}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime\prime}}

for some ground type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.85}{{B}}}
.

[R-Cond-T]

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime\prime}}
.

[R-Cond-F]

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime\prime}}
.

Deriving Session Fidelity.

We derive session fidelity of the top-down system.
We use the soundness and completeness of the association with
session fidelity of the bottom-up system

Theorem 30

(Session Fidelity,
[
29
, Theorem 4.13]
)
.

Assume
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash^{\text{\tiny bot}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}M}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}}}

with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}
live. Assume
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\to}}
.
Then there exist
M^{\prime}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime\prime}}
such that

M\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\to}}}^{+}M^{\prime}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime\prime}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\to}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime\prime}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant_{\text{a}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}
,
and

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash^{\text{\tiny bot}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}M^{\prime}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime}}}}
.

Theorem 31

(Session Fidelity of the Top-Down System)
.

Assume
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash^{\text{\tiny top}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}M}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}}
is derived by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{a}}{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}\,{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\xrightarrow{\phantom{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\alpha}}}}
. Then there exist

M^{\prime}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}
such that

M\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\to}}}^{+}M^{\prime}
,

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}\,{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\xrightarrow{\phantom{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\alpha}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}^{\prime}
, and

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash^{\text{\tiny top}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}M^{\prime}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime}}}}
with

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{a}}{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}}}
.

Proof.

We prove the following stronger statement, by which the above theorem
is derived.

Assume
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash^{\text{\tiny top}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}M}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}}
is derived by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{a}}{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}\,{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\xrightarrow{\phantom{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\alpha}}}}
. Then there exist

M^{\prime}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime\prime}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}
such that

M\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\to}}}^{+}M^{\prime}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime\prime}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\to}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime\prime}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant_{\text{a}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}
,

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}\,{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\xrightarrow{\phantom{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\alpha}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}^{\prime}
, and

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash^{\text{\tiny top}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}M^{\prime}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime}}}}
with

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{a}}{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}}}
.

Assume

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{a}}{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}}
. By the soundness of the association,

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}\,{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\xrightarrow{\phantom{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\alpha}}}}
implies

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\to}}
.
Suppose
M\equiv\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{dom}\!\left({\Delta}\right)}}{({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\ |\ {\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft h_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}})}

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash^{\text{\tiny top}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}M^{\prime}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}}

is derived with
(
69
) above.
By
Thm.

30
,
there exist
M^{\prime}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime\prime}}
such that

M\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\to}}}^{+}M^{\prime}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime\prime}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\to}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime\prime}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant_{\text{a}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}
.
Hence by the completeness of the association,
and
Thm.

29
,

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}\,{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\xrightarrow{\phantom{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\alpha}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{a}}{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}}}
with

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash^{\text{\tiny top}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}M^{\prime}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta^{\prime}}}}
, as desired.
∎

Remark 9

(No Session Fidelity Labels)
.

Unlike
Remark

8
,
we cannot identify the rules used in
the session reduction
from the initial global transition.
We cannot even identify
the participant undertaking
the reduction!
The reason is the same as in
Remark

5
,
the asynchronous subtyping
allows us to reorganise participants’ actions.
Therefore, the global type transition
may reflect an action
that can be brought forward by
supertyping,
but that the session cannot do right now.
Regardless,
the concluding transitions
are induced by
Thm.

29
,
hence the labels can be deduced
from
Remark

8
.

Next we show that typed multiparty sessions
are live.

Theorem 32

(Session Liveness,
[
29
, Theorem 4.12]
)
.

Assume
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash^{\text{\tiny bot}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}M}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}}}

with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}
live.
Then
M
is live.

Theorem 33

(Liveness of the Top-Down System)
.

Assume
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash^{\text{\tiny top}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}M}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}}
. Then,
M
is safe, deadlock-free
and live.

Proof.

We first note that if
M
is live, then
M
is safe and
deadlock-free.
If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{a}}{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}
is live
by
Thm.

13

and we have that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash^{\text{\tiny bot}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}M}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}}

as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash^{\text{\tiny top}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}M}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}}
.
Then by
Thm.

32
,
M
is live.
∎

Example 17

(Program Requiring Message Reordering)
.

Consider a program that may exhibit
different behaviours depending on non-deterministic control flow;
this may require the precise asynchronous subtyping to type,
since the different behaviours may need to be reconciled via
message reordering.

Recall
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{\text{\tiny non-det}}}
from
Ex.

7
,
its projections,
and their subtypes:

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{\text{\tiny non-det}}}={\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}{\to}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}{:}\left\{{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}_{1}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}{\to}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}_{1}}}}\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathbf{t}}}}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}_{2}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}{\to}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}_{2}}}}\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}{\to}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}}}}}}}}\end{array}}\right\}}}}

(70)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}}}\oplus\!\left\{{\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}1}}}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}2}}}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}}\end{array}}\right\}}}}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}}\&\!\left\{{\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}1}}}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}1}}}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}2}}}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}2}}}}\end{array}}\right\}}}}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}}}\&\!\left\{{\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}1}}}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}2}}}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\&}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}}\end{array}}\right\}}}}

(71)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{n}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}}}\,\underbrace{\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}1}}}}\dots\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}1}}}}}_{n}\,\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}2}}}}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\oplus}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}1}}}}\mathbin{\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}.}\!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathbf{t}}}}}

(72)

We can use these to type a protocol that
cannot be typed without the precise asynchronous subtyping.
Suppose that
e_{1}
and
e_{2}

are non-deterministic boolean typed expressions.

\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}=\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{if}\,e_{1}\,\mathbf{then}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}\mathord{!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\mathord{!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}2}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{else\,if}\,e_{2}\,\mathbf{then}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}\mathord{!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\mathord{!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}1}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\mathord{!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}2}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{else}\,}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}.{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\mathord{!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}1}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}}\end{array}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}.{\sum\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\mathord{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}1}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}\mathord{!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}1}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\mathord{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}2}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}\mathord{!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}2}}\end{array}\right\}}}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}.{\sum\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\mathord{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}1}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\mathord{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}2}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\mathord{?}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}\end{array}\right\}}}\end{array}

(73)

We can derive the following type judgements without using subtyping:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}}}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}\mathord{!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\mathord{!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}2}}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{0}}}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}\mathord{!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\mathord{!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}1}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\mathord{!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}2}}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{1}}}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}.{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\mathord{!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}1}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}}

(74)

We have that, for all
n\geq 0
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{n}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant_{\text{a}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T^{\prime}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant_{\text{a}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}
,
so, using
[T-Sub]
, we get the following type judgements:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}\mathord{!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\mathord{!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}2}}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}\mathord{!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\mathord{!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}1}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\mathord{!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}2}}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}.{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\mathord{!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}1}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}

(75)

Hence, we can type
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}
,
allowing us to type the session using
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{\text{\tiny non-det}}}
:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}\triangleleft\epsilon\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\triangleleft\epsilon\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}\triangleleft\epsilon\right)}\triangleright{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}}}\mathpunct{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9},}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}}}\mathpunct{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9},}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}\mathbin{\!:\!}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}}}}\right)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\sqsubseteq}_{a}}{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}_{\text{\tiny non-det}}}}}}

(76)

Therefore, the session is safe, deadlock-free and live by
Thm.

33
.
If our subtyping did not include asynchronous message reordering,
then we would be unable to reconcile the control flows of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}
,
making it untypable.

We use this session to witness the utility of the
non-deterministic global transition relation,
as in
Ex.

7
,
by considering the three possible control flows:

\begin{array}[]{clclclcl}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}&\triangleleft&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}}&\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}&\triangleleft&\epsilon\\
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}&\triangleleft&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}&\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}&\triangleleft&\epsilon\\
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}&\triangleleft&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}}&\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}&\triangleleft&\epsilon\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{clclclcl}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}&\triangleleft&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}\mathord{!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\mathord{!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}2}}&\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}&\triangleleft&\epsilon\\
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}&\triangleleft&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}&\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}&\triangleleft&\epsilon\\
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}&\triangleleft&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}}&\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}&\triangleleft&\epsilon\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{clclclcl}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}&\triangleleft&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}\mathord{!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\mathord{!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}1}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\mathord{!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}2}}&\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}&\triangleleft&\epsilon\\
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}&\triangleleft&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}&\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}&\triangleleft&\epsilon\\
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}&\triangleleft&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}}&\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}&\triangleleft&\epsilon\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{clclclcl}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}&\triangleleft&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mu{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}.{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}\mathord{!}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\mathtt{m}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}1}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\!.\!}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}}}&\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}&\triangleleft&\epsilon\\
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}&\triangleleft&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}}&\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}&\triangleleft&\epsilon\\
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}&\triangleleft&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}}&\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid}}&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}&\triangleleft&\epsilon\end{array}

*
*
*

All three sessions are typed by
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{\text{\tiny non-det}}}
,
but, after
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
enqueues the next message to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}

in the left and right sessions,
the session on the left can be typed by

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}

while the session on the right cannot be. Where:

{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}{\to}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}_{2}}}}\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}}{\to}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}_{2}}}}\vphantom{x}\!\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}.}}\!{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}}{\overset{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}}{\rightsquigarrow}}{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{r}}}}}}{:}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}\mathtt{m}}}}}}}}}}}}

This explains why the transitions of
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G_{\text{\tiny non-det}}}

allow for non-determinism:
it needs to handle all processes of the above form
(with any number of communications from
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{p}}}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\mathtt{q}}}}}
),
which no single transition can handle simultaneously.

7
Related Work

Preciseness of Subtyping.

For synchronous programs, two subtyping approaches have been
proposed: one allowing the safe substitution of channels (called
channel subtyping
)
[
27
]
and
the other allowing the safe substitution of processes (called
process subtyping
)
[
19
]
. In this
work, we use process subtyping,
which represents refinement of process behaviours.

Preciseness of subtyping
was first proposed for the call-by-value

\lambda
-calculus with iso-recursive types by Ligatti et al.
[
42
]
.
Chen et al.
[
13
]
introduced
and proved
preciseness
of
synchronous
[
27
,
19
]
and asynchronous subtyping for
the binary (2-party) session types.
Later, the asynchronous subtyping
was found to be
undecidable
, independently by Bravetti et
al.
[
8
]
,
and by Lange and Yoshida
[
37
]
.
This provoked active studies on identifying
a set of binary session types where asynchronous subtyping
is decidable; and proposing
sound
algorithms

[
7
,
4
]

extending the formalism to
binary
communicating automata

[
6
]
.
A variant of asynchronous binary session types
based on service contract theory, called
the
fair
asynchronous subtyping relation, was also proposed and
proved sound and complete in

[
9
]
.

In the multiparty setting,
Ghilezan et al.
[
28
,
29
]

proposed precise synchronous and asynchronous
session subtyping, and our theory is
centred on precise multiparty asynchronous subtyping.
Recently, Bocchi et al.
[
5
]

have shown a sound algorithm for asynchronous multiparty session types
that can decide subtyping
for non-trivial multiparty communication patterns, extending
an abstract interpretation framework from Bocchi et al
[
4
]
.
Li et al.
[
38
]
proposed
a sound and complete algorithm
for asynchronous behavioural contract refinement
subtyping on communicating state machines (CSMs)
[
6
]
,
which is distinct from the precise asynchronous subtyping:
as it is defined to be complete with respect to subprotocol fidelity.
As noted in
[
4
, § 7]
, classically, the subtyping
question is answered pairwise: by comparing a type against a candidate
subtype, without reference to a global type. Our classical formulation
is favourable when types are mined from source code, allowing

local
optimisations of specifications and code per participant.
See the paragraph on
Implementations
below.

Top-Down Asynchronous Systems.

The first multiparty session types system

[
34
,
35
]
(MPST)
introduced
asynchronous multiparty session processes
which interact through
FIFO queues. The typing system uses
the most restricted end-point projection (called an
inductive projection with plain merging)
and does not consider subtyping. Later this projection was extended to
an inductive projection with full merging with synchronous subtyping
relations, which is used in several asynchronous MPST systems
(e.g.,
[
22
]
).
Scalas and Yoshida have discovered that proofs of
an inductive projection with full merging in the literature
are flawed
[
51
]
.
Recently this proof was fixed by Hou and Yoshida
[
36
]
for
a
synchronous calculus
, using
an association relation between a global type and typing
context up to the synchronous subtyping relation.
We apply their proof method to an asynchronous
calculus up to the precise asynchronous subtyping,
with a coinductive projection with full merging; the coinductive full merge subsumes the inductive full merge of earlier MPST systems, since any inductively-derivable merge also satisfies our coinductive rules. Hence
our type system offers strictly larger typable processes than those typed
by the association relation in
[
36
]
.
Barbanera and Dezani-Ciancaglini
[
2
]

extend global types with syntax for delegation
and separate the sending and receiving syntax,
allowing some of the expressivity given by the precise asynchronous
subtyping.
They give a decidable type system directly
between sessions and global types,
without using a projection.
Notably, their global types do not include en-route transmissions
and are not given semantics;
instead they define
type configurations
,
which are pairs of global types and queues,
and give these semantics.
Unlike
Thms.

29
and
31
, their subject reduction
and session fidelity theorems
refer to type configurations
not
global types.
This subsumes points (1) and (2)
from
Def.

16
, and
avoids point (3) since
messages can be globally dequeued
at only point,
independent of the structure of the global type.

Asynchronous local types are often viewed as
Communicating Finite State Machines (CFSMs)
[
6
]
.
The work
[
20
]
which first studied
a connection between multiparty local types and CFSMs
proposed a graphical asynchronous
calculus with mixed choice which is typed by a graphical global type
with mixed choice and parallel composition.
After this work, researchers started studying properties
of global types or global protocols using the theory of
CFSMs and applied their theories to implementations of MPSTs,
see
[
60
]
. Extending methodologies of message sequence charts (MSC),
recent work focus on
implementability
or
realisability
–whether there is a
CFSM implementation of
the specified global protocol (or MSC)

[
44
,
39
,
52
,
23
]
.
Their approach aims to produce decidable,
sound and complete (with respect to traces)
projections from sender-driven global types
(or global state machines) to CFSMs.

While CFSMs related session type works only focus on
CFSMs (and global protocols),
recent work in
[
53
]

has proposed Protocol State Machines (PSMs) and
Automata-based Multiparty Protocols (AMP)
as an alternative framework for
asynchronous message passing processes.
Their PSMs project onto CFSMs,
which are then used to type processes,
similar to how global types project onto
local types.
While a typing context projected from
a global type (modulo the synchronous subtyping) can only produce
1-synchronous
and half-duplex behaviours (as proven
multiparty
compatibility
in
[
21
]
),
their work
[
20
,
53
]

and ours present more expressive global type behaviours.
By extending en-route transmissions notation
and context transmission rules for global types,
our global and local type semantics do not impose half-duplex
limitations.

The most important distinction between all of the above CFSM systems
and ours is the property of
liveness
;
using well-formed global types enforces liveness in the presence of
single selection and conditional processes (features of the original session
calculi
[
54
,
32
]
),
while the calculi from
[
20
,
53
]

exclude these
constructs.
In our system,
liveness is downward closed with respect to
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant_{\text{a}}}}

as proven in
[
29
, Lemma 4.10]
.
See
[
51
, Example 5.14]

for a counterexample which demonstrates liveness without
the fair path assumption (weaker liveness)
is
not
downward closed with respect to
subtyping relations, thus the typing system is unsound under their weaker
liveness (which corresponds to orphan message freedom in

[
20
,
21
,
53
]
).
In contrast, we have proven that the
coinductive projection with full merging
is sound and complete with respect to asynchronous trace reordering,
demonstrating the correctness of the top-down
MPST with asynchronous precise multiparty session subtyping relation.

Implementations of Asynchronous Multiparty Session
Subtyping.

Asynchronous session subtyping was first introduced for
messaging optimisation in session-based high-performance
computing platforms,
i.e., multicore C programming
[
33
,
59
]
and MPI-C
[
47
,
46
]
.
Castro-Perez and Yoshida
[
12
]
proposed CAMP, which is a static
performance analysis framework for message-passing concurrent and
distributed systems based on MPST. CAMP augments MPST with
annotations of communication latency and local computation cost,
defined as estimated execution times, which is used to extract cost
equations from protocol descriptions and to statically predict the
communication cost. CAMP is also extended to analyse asynchronous communication optimised programs. The tool, based on cost
theory, is applicable to different existing benchmarks and usecases
in the literature with a wide range of communication protocols.

The Rust programming framework, Rumpsteak
[
18
]
,
incorporates multiparty asynchronous subtyping to optimise
asynchronous message-passing in the Rust programming language.
It proposes an algorithm for asynchronous subtyping
based on the session decomposition technique that is
bounded by a number of iterations and proved to be sound and
decidable. They evaluate the performance and expressiveness of
Rumpsteak against previous Rust implementations and
algorithms, and show that
Rumpsteak is more efficient and can safely implement case studies
by offering arbitrary ordering of messages.

Mechanisations of Asynchronous Subtyping Relations.

Hinrichsen et al.
[
31
]
introduce Actris, a Iris Rocq that
integrates separation logics and asynchronous binary session types
with the asynchronous subtyping by Chen et al.
[
13
]
.
The first mechanisation of multiparty asynchronous subtyping
by the Rocq proof assistant is proposed by Ekici and Yoshida
[
25
,
26
]
.
Their Rocq formalisation follows
a session tree decomposition approach,
using the greatest fixed point of the least fixed point technique,
facilitated by the paco library, to define coinductive predicates.
Li et al.
[
41
]
have formalised
correctness of their implementation problem
of refinement global specifications
[
40
]
in Rocq.
It is an interesting future work to combine it with
the Rocq mechanisation of the top-down approach by Ekici et al.
[
24
]
,
to formalise the subject reduction, deadlock-freedom and
liveness theorems based on this work.

8
Conclusion

We have proposed an asynchronous association relation
and proved its sound and complete operational correspondence.
This work is the first to prove these results based on
(1) asynchronous precise subtyping and (2) projection with
coinductive full merging. We introduced a new operational semantics
for global types, which captures more behaviours allowed by permuting actions
than the previous asynchronous global type
semantics provided by Barwell et al.
[
3
]
and Honda et al.
[
35
]
.
We developed a new projection relation which associates global types with
a pair of a local type and a queue type for each participant.
Using this correspondence, we derived
the subject reduction theorem and
the session fidelity theorem of the top-down system
from the corresponding theorems of the bottom-up system

[
29
, Theorem 4.11 and 4.13]
.
By proving that the projection
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\Delta}

of
{\color[rgb]{0.43,0.21,0.1}G}
is safe, deadlock-free and live, we can derive that asynchronous
multiparty session processes typed by
the top-down typing system (
[SessTop]
)
are also safe, deadlock-free and live (Theorem
33
).

While Ghilezan et al.
[
29
]
have proved the subject reduction theorem
and session fidelity theorem under the subsumption rule
of
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant_{\text{a}}}}
, it does not use the top-down typing system.
On the other hand, Ghilezan et al.
[
28
]
have shown that multiparty synchronous
subtyping is precise in the synchronous multiparty session calculus
using the top-down system. None of the previous works
has studied:
(1) properties of coinductive full merge projection;
(2) liveness of asynchronously associated typing contexts; nor
(3) association with respect to asynchronous subtyping.
An interesting open question is whether the
association theorems hold for the sound decidable asynchronous subtyping
relations
[
18
,
5
]

so that we can derive the subject reduction theorems under those
relations.

Pischke and Yoshida
[
50
]
have shown that in
synchronous semantics, the top-down system has the same typability
as the bottom-up system, which ensures liveness,
if the top-down system meets three conditions:
(1) global types are balanced;
(2) the projection is coinductive with full
merging; and
(3) the subsumption rule uses
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant}}
.
We have proven the correctness of the bottom-up
system with
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.9}\leqslant_{\text{a}}}}
implies
the correctness of the top-down system where
(1) a global
type is
\text{balanced}^{+}
; (2) a projection is coinductive with full
merging (soundness of typability).
It is an open question whether our top-down system can

completely
type sessions which are typable by the
bottom-up system, which ensures asynchronous liveness
(completeness of typability).

We have demonstrated
the usefulness of association in deriving the main theorems of the
top-down system, by
reusing
the theorems in
[
29
]
.
We have not yet reached a stage to claim that
MPST is a theoretical framework for building asynchronous
software systems.
There still needs to be more effort applied to developing practical applications of MPST with asynchronous subtyping relations,
for testing and maintaining compositionality and reusability of
protocols.
The most challenging topic is to type individual
asynchronous
components
,
each being written in a different programming
language or running on a different platform,
while ensuring their type-safety and deadlock-freedom,
assuming they conform to a shared global protocol.
Implementing such a distributed architecture requires
significant engineering effort such as defining system
requirements, identifying components,
splitting the system into components,
integrating these components, and designing the interfaces for
components. We will continue our avenue to promote
asynchronous optimisations with MPST for specifying and implementing
real-world distributed components.

Acknowledgements.

We thank the reviewers for their detailed
comments and helpful suggestions.
